5541 ay Li aad JS] Lip) Bulaead | plj5al weld 1 lala Jyudly pal ons JA y Ge LAS oa  ob Sud gd} ga at Jolly wantdl gn Talay ly Lally Ulaad) pd 580 Gi Su JM UN alu)  AS Ulaadly poled) JI Jems) &glaslly Galant) = Ula ols Jin Las
(15.1) M. HARB (Liban) (traduit de l'arabe) :  Monsieur le Président, Monsieur le Directeur général, Excellences, Mesdames et Messieurs les  membres des délégations, Mesdames, Messieurs, permettez-moi tout d'abord, Monsieur le Président, de vous  féliciter pour votre élection à la présidence de cette vingt-sixième session de la Conférence générale de  l'UNESCO. Je suis convaincu que votre longue expérience et la sagesse avec laquelle vous dirigez les séances  meneront cette Conférence aux résultats les meilleurs et les plus efficaces possibles au service des objectifs de  notre Organisation. Je voudrais également saluer les Etats qui ont rejoint l'UNESCO au début de cette session, à  savoir l'Estonie, la Lituanie, la Lettonie et Tuvalu.
(15.2) Je viens d'un pays qui s'est voué à la science et à la culture, ainsi qu'au dialogue et à l'interaction  positive entre les civilisations et entre les peuples, un pays qu'une guerre destructrice a failli priver de son rôle et  de sa place au sein des nations. Mais je suis heureux aujourd'hui, heureux de vous rencontrer alors que le Liban a  tourné la page de la violence et a pris la résolution d'édifier la paix avec ardeur et constance et de reprendre sa  place dans le concert des nations. Il demeure ainsi une terre de convergence culturelle, un phare du savoir, un  pays qui a pour vocation de propager la connaissance et la créativité parmi les peuples.
(15.3) La vérité et la loyauté me commandent de remercier les pays amis et frères et les organisations  internationales, tout particulièrement l'UNESCO, qui ont aidé le Liban à traverser cette difficile épreuve. Le  processus de paix au Liban en est encore à ses débuts. Il a pour garantie la détermination du Liban de le mener à  bien et s'appuie sur le soutien constant et résolu des institutions internationales et des Etats amis. Les premières  manifestations de ce soutien international ont sans doute été la décision de rouvrir le Bureau régional d'éducation  de l'UNESCO pour les Etats arabes à Beyrouth en avril 1992 et l'envoi de missions pluridisciplinaires au Liban.  Au nom du gouvernement libanais, et en mon nom personnel, je tiens à renouveler mes remerciements au  Directeur général, car cette décision a fait une excellente impression aussi bien sur le peuple que sur le  gouvernement libanais.
(15.4) Monsieur le Président, Mesdames, Messieurs, au Liban, nous comprenons très bien le sens des mots  paix, justice et grande liberté et nous y attachons un grand prix. Toutes ces notions appartiennent à nos traditions  historiques et culturelles. Or, aujourd'hui nous en avons d'autant plus conscience et nous y sommes d'autant plus  attachés que nous avons connu les malheurs de la guerre. Le Liban, qui a tourné la page de la guerre, regarde  avec effroi la destruction de ses infrastructures, engage la communauté internationale à tirer les leçons de son
14   amère expérience pour que les peuples défendent leur souveraineté et leur droit à une vie libre et à  l'autodétermination. C'est là en effet que se manifestent l'héroïsme des peuples et leur détermination à vivre  dignement. C'est en donnant leur sens à ces mots que nous pourrons aborder le XXIe siècle confiants et assurés  que l'époque des tragédies est définitivement révolue.
(15.5) Mon pays a pleinement conscience de ce que le processus de paix est un chantier semé d'obstacles et de  difficultés. Mais nous sommes résolus à travailler, et la volonté de vivre de notre peuple est immense et capable  d'aplanir tous les obstacles et de fournir toutes les énergies nécessaires pour mener à bien la reconstruction de  notre identité sur des bases solides, permettant au Liban de rejoindre la communauté internationale autour des  valeurs humaines universelles que nous nous employons tous à revivifier, à consolider et à propager. Sur la base  de ces considérations, nous avons pensé que la construction commence par l'éducation, qui est le fondement de la  stabilité sociale et nationale. Nous avons fixé un objectif principal que nous nous efforçons de réaliser  progressivement et qui consiste à assurer l'éducation à tous. Nous avons pris l'engagement d'atteindre cet objectif  dans le cadre de l'entente nationale réalisée à la Conférence de Taëf, qui fait de la scolarité obligatoire un objectif  national dans le domaine de l'éducation. Cet objectif exige le renforcement de l'éducation formelle, sur le plan  aussi bien quantitatif que qualitatif, à tous les niveaux - du jardin d'enfants à l'université. Nous avons également  dû consentir des dépenses importantes et imprévues pour assurer la réhabilitation des constructions scolaires  détruites et les équiper afin qu'elles puissent accueillir le plus grand nombre possible d'élèves au début de cette  année.
(15.6) Le gouvernement d'entente nationale s'est penché sur les programmes d'études en vue de les revoir de  fond en comble, de façon à les adapter au progrès scientifique et culturel récent, d'une part, et à donner aux  valeurs humaines et à l'éducation civique la place éminente qui leur revient, d'autre part. Comme nous avons été  heureux d'entendre le Directeur général insister, dans son discours inaugural, sur la dimension éthique du  développement ! Nous avons aussi entrepris de réformer de fond en comble l'administration publique de  l'éducation afin de la moderniser et de la rendre plus efficace. Dans ce cadre, nous avons commencé à collaborer  avec une mission d'experts envoyée par l'UNESCO et le gouvernement français pour étudier le moyen  d'informatiser les services de cette administration de façon à en améliorer l'efficacité et à en rationaliser le travail.
(15.7) Par ailleurs, nous nous sommes fixé comme objectif principal de renforcer l'enseignement technique et  professionnel et nous avons entrepris de réformer et de développer l'ensemble de ce secteur. Nous savons que cet  enseignement coûte plus cher que les autres et qu'il répond à un besoin plus pressant car il garantit une  productivité plus grande et un lien plus étroit avec les besoins de la société. Nous sommes heureux que  l'orientation du programme de l'UNESCO pour les deux années à venir concorde avec l'orientation de notre pays  dans ce domaine. À cet égard, nous attachons une importance particulière au projet visant à réhabiliter et à  renforcer le centre d'océanographie de Batroun afin d'en faire un centre modèle d'enseignement professionnel  supérieur, capable de répondre aux besoins d'ordre humain et économique et de rehausser le prestige de  l'enseignement professionnel aux yeux du public. Nous sommes tout à fait persuadés que l'UNESCO accordera à  ce centre l'attention qu'il mérite.
(15.8) S'agissant de la culture, l'intérêt manifesté par l'UNESCO pour nos institutions culturelles nous a aidés à  poursuivre nos projets de sauvegarde et de protection de ces institutions. Nous nous félicitons de la décision qu'a  prise le Directeur général concernant la participation de l'Organisation à la reconstruction du Musée national de  Beyrouth. Nous nous réjouissons aussi de ce que l'Organisation souhaite nous associer à la recherche de sources  de financement pour ce projet. Je suis heureux d'annoncer que le Liban s'apprête aujourd'hui à mettre en place un  Ministère de la culture qui gérera les efforts rapides que nous faisons pour renforcer notre rôle culturel dans le  monde. Par ailleurs, au nom du gouvernement libanais et en mon nom personnel, je tiens à remercier  l'Organisation de l'intérêt qu'elle porte à la question de la protection des sites historiques et archéologiques du  Liban et tout particulièrement des vestiges de la ville de Tyr. Je souhaite que soit adopté le projet de résolution  soumis à la Conférence générale en vue du lancement d'une campagne internationale de sauvegarde du site  archéologique de Tyr et de l'appui à une campagne de lutte contre les fouilles illicites menées au Liban et  l'exportation illicite d'objets archéologiques. Le gouvernement libanais se déclare prêt, si ce projet de résolution  est adopté, à mettre tous les moyens qu'il possède à la disposition de l'UNESCO pour assurer le succès de cette  campagne.
(15.9) Le Liban, vous le savez, occupe une place éminente sur le plan archéologique. Tous nos monuments,  dans toutes les régions du pays, à Baalbek comme à Byblos, Sidon, Tripoli, Beït el Dine et ailleurs,  n'appartiennent pas aux seuls Libanais, mais sont un élément du patrimoine mondial et il est de notre devoir à  tous de coopérer pour en empêcher l'exportation, le transfert ou le commerce illicites, conformément à la  Convention adoptée par l'Organisation et à laquelle le Liban a adhéré en 1991. L'UNESCO a un rôle important à  jouer dans le retour à leur lieu d'origine des éléments de notre patrimoine qui ont été dérobés.
(15.10) Monsieur le Président, le gouvernement libanais tient à exprimer sa profonde reconnaissance à  l'UNESCO pour l'intérêt qu'elle porte à la question de la création d'un "Centre international pour les sciences de  l'homme et le développement” à Byblos (Jebaïl) au Liban. Notre pays a conclu en 1973 un accord préalable avec
546   l'UNESCO concernant ce centre. Malheureusement, la dégradation de la situation au Liban a conduit à  abandonner le projet. Aujourd'hui que la paix est rétablie sur le territoire national, nous nous félicitons de  l'attention que le Directeur général accorde à ce projet, que nous espérons voir reprendre vie dans un proche  avenir. Nous sommes, quant à nous, prêts à signer un nouvel accord avec l'Organisation à ce sujet et à mobiliser  tous les moyens dont nous disposons pour en assurer le succès.
(15.11) Monsieur le Président, Mesdames, Messieurs, nous avons depuis un an et quelque accompli au Liban  des pas certains sur la voie de la paix. Mais nous avançons remplis d'appréhension, un oeil sur le Liban et l'autre  sur ce qui se passe dans le monde. Pas un seul jour nous n'avons oublié les tracas du monde, même aux heures les  plus sombres ct les plus rudes, L'homme est notre frère où qu'il soit, nous compatissons à sa souffrance, nous nous  réjouissons de ce qui le réjouit, nous sommes solidaires avec lui dans le bonheur comme dans le malheur et nous  soutenons son combat pour les valeurs humaines, que nous nous efforçons tous d'affermir et de défendre. Même  au comble de son malheur, notre peuple s'est toujours senti concerné par ce qui se passait dans les autres pays, ce  qui touchait les autres peuples. Nos regards étaient fixés sur les autres foyers de tension dans le monde et nous  avons suivi avec satisfaction l'évolution des société d'Europe de l'Est vers l'instauration de plus de liberté et de  démocratie et le renforcement des droits de l'homme dans ces Etats. Nos 1egards étaient également fixés sur les  troubles qui ont secoué la région du Golfe et la guerre qui y a éclaté a suscité en nous des inquiétudes. Nous  attendons avec espoir que la paix soit rétablie dans cette région sur la base du respect de la souveraineté nationale  des différents peuples, que les droits de l'individu y soient protégés et qu'y soient éliminées les séquelles de la  guerre.
(15.12) Nous attachons à la Conférence internationale sur la paix au Moyen-Orient une importance décisive et y  voyons l'amorce d'un processus de paix dans notre région et dans le monde. C'est pourquoi le Liban participera à  cette Conférence en arborant le drapeau du droit et de la justice pour que soit rendu au peuple palestinien son  droit à l'autodétermination et à la création de son Etat sur son territoire national, et pour la libération des  territoires arabes occupés et en particulier du Sud-Liban, cher à notre coeur, et du Golan, conformément aux  résolutions internationales adoptées à ce sujet. Les organisations internationales ne sont pas un mur des  lamentations vers lequel se tournent les faibles et les opprimés, mais elles doivent être capables de faire  appliquer leurs décisions afin que les peuples recouvrent leurs droits et leur foi dans l'idée que le nouvel ordre  mondial reposera sur le droit, la souveraineté nationale, la justice et la liberté.
(15.13) Monsieur le Président, Mesdames, Messieurs, le gouvernement libanais appuie le Projet de programme  et de budget proposé par le Directeur général pour 1992-1993. Il approuve aussi toute réforme des méthodes de  travail destinée à améliorer l'efficacité de l'Organisation dans ce cadre. Nous souscrivons à l'idée d'accroître  l'efficacité du Conseil exécutif et soutenons le principe du consensus qui a été suivi jusqu'ici. Nous formons des  voeux pour que notre Organisation soit plus créative sur le plan des idées, plus efficace dans le domaine de  Yalphabétisation et plus soucieuse de la pertinence dans le cadre de projets ciblés relevant des différents  domaines du programme.
(15.14) En ce qui concerne les priorités définies par l'UNESCO, nous les appuyons d'une manière générale,  mais nous voudrions formuler quelques observations à ce sujet. Nous approuvons l'importance accordée aux  sciences et à l'environnement dans le programme de l'Organisation, mais nous pensons qu'il faudrait adopter une  approche globale et intégrée de la question de la protection et de la défense de l'environnement, et donc lier le  progrès scientifique et technologique à l'environnement et à son intégrité, de telle façon que, grâce à ce progrès,  les êtres humains puissent vivre plus en sécurité et mieux.
(15.15) Quant à la question des femmes et du renforcement des possibilités qui leur sont offertes de participer  aux différents domaines de la vie, nous saluons les efforts particuliers déployés par l'Organisation à cet effet.  Nous avons mis cette question au centre de nos priorités au niveau national et nous espérons que l'UNESCO nous  apportera son aide dans ce domaine.
(15.16) S'agissant de l'aide que l'UNESCO apporte aux pays africains et aux pays les moins avancés, nous  considérons qu'il faudrait ajouter à cette catégorie les Etats victimes de catastrophes dues à l'action de l'homme où  que ce soit dans le monde. Nous engageons le Directeur général à accorder à ces Etats une attention particulière  dans le cadre du Programme ordinaire et du Programme de participation.
(15.17) Nous savions déjà qu'il ne pourrait y avoir de paix sans liberté, ni de liberté sans développement.  Ajoutons à cela qu'il ne peut y avoir ni paix, ni liberté, ni développement sans solidarité entre les peuples et entre  les nations, fondée sur le respect de la souveraineté nationale de chaque peuple. Là réside le rôle essentiel de  l'UNESCO, qui consiste à répandre les valeurs de justice, de liberté et d'humanisme parmi les peuples et les  Etats. Dans cette tâche, nous sommes aux côtés de l'UNESCO. Que notre devise soit : solidarité et coopération  pour parvenir à la paix et à la justice. Je vous remercie.
16. The PRESIDENT:  I thank the head of the delegation of Lebanon. I now give the floor to H.E. Ambassador Jean Soneriu,  Permanent Observer of the Sovereign Military Order of Malta to UNESCO.
17.1 M. SONERIU (Ordre souverain de Malte) :  Monsieur le Président de la Conférence générale, Monsieur le Président du Conseil exécutif, Monsieur  le Directeur général, Mesdames et Messieurs, permettez-moi tout d'abord, Monsieur le Président, de vous  adresser, au nom de ma délégation de l'Ordre souverain de Malte, mes plus sincères félicitations pour votre  élection à la vingt-sixième session de la Conférence générale de l'UNESCO.
17.2 — La nouvelle décennie qui nous sépare du XXIe siècle est marquée par plusieurs défis qui interpellent  notre courage et qui nécessitent des réponses à l'attente des peuples qui ont les yeux tournés vers nous. Le  principal défi est la prolifération démentielle de l'espèce humaine, suivant l'expression du savant ethnologue  Lévi-Strauss. Cette explosion démographique à laquelle il est fait allusion a pour conséquence immédiate la  déforestation. Tel est le cas en Amazonie, où des milliers d'hectares d'arbres sont abattus. Or, comme il est  généralement admis, la forêt véhicule l'oxygène que nous respirons, provoque les pluies qui irriguent notre sol  et sans lesquelles celui-ci devient un désert aride, avec toutes les conséquences imprévisibles qui en résultent.  Le continent africain, l'Inde, offrent l'exemple de cette explosion dont les effets sont dès à présent visibles,  Ainsi l'on constate que Madras, en Inde, est un centre de vente d'organes humains. Certains pays de l'Amérique  Latine offrent le triste exemple de la vente des enfants, qui font l'objet parfois d'un marchandage honteux.
17.3 Au cours des dernières sessions de la Conférence générale, vous vous êtes penchés sur le problème de  l'apartheid et de la pollution. Votre souci principal était la sauvegarde de la région de l'Antarctique afin  d'écarter tout danger de pollution. Une convention de Melbourne menaçait cette région d'une possibilité  d'exploitation minière de nature à entraîner fatalement la pollution de cette partie du monde, qui était à l'abri de  ce danger. Après de multiples efforts, la cause fut gagnée et l'Antarctique fut sauvée du péril qui la menaçait,  pour le plus grand bien de l'humanité.
17.4 Dans un autre domaine, afin d'enrichir la mémoire de l'humanité, vous avez porté vos efforts sur les  fouilles archéologiques réalisées dans la ville de Jérusalem ; vous vous êtes alors heurtés à certaines difficultés  qui, nous l'espérons, pourront être aplanies.
17.5 Un autre défi nous interpelle. Le vent de la liberté souffle sur une grande partie du continent européen  qui a subi le joug totalitaire pendant plus de 70 ans, au cours desquels la faillite a provoqué le désarroi de ses  peuples disséminés à travers le monde. Leur besoin d'aide alimentaire, de secours matériel, de support moral  nécessite d'urgence la mise en oeuvre de mesures de secours médical et d'approvisionnement de tout genre pour  permettre aux nouveau-nés surtout, que ce soit en Roumanie, en Yougoslavie ou ailleurs, de surmonter le  danger de mort par malnutrition.
176 Un autre problème se pose également à l'attention de l'UNESCO, c'est celui de l'enseignement et de  l'éducation. Pendant de nombreuses années, par les soins de ses représentants, par l'implantation de centres  culturels et d'écoles, l'UNESCO a réussi à combattre l'ignorance et à répandre le fruit précieux de la culture.
17.7 L'Ordre souverain de Malte, toujours fidèle à ses nobles objectifs, n'a pas attendu les récents  bouleversements survenus dans l'Europe de l'Est pour organiser, et ceci déjà depuis de nombreuses années, une  aide intensive sous forme d'envoi de médicaments, de matériel hospitalier, de nourriture, de missions  médicales, etc. Il était présent en particulier à Tchernobyl et en Géorgie. Cette activité de l'Ordre devrait  s'intensifier grâce à l'établissement de relations diplomatiques avec la Hongrie, la Pologne, la Roumanie ct la  Tchécoslovaquie. Des pourparlers sont actuellement en cours avec d'autres pays de l'Est.
17.8 Tous ces efforts de l'Ordre sont secondés par le concours offert dans le cadre de l'exécution des  résolutions adoptées par l'UNESCO. Mais ce stade se trouve aujourd'hui dépassé car le chômage qui a pris  racine et s'est amplifié dans plusieurs pays européens a jeté sur le marché des centaines de milliers de jeunes  angoissés, désespérés, ne trouvant pas un travail quelconque. C'est ainsi que se pose le dilemme consistant à  concilier l'enseignement prodigué jusqu'à présent avec l'impératif des formations techniques, afin que puissent  arriver sur le marché du travail des jeunes gens qualifiés pour occuper des postes dans des entreprises qui en  ont besoin, étant donné la compétitivité des agents capables de faire face à ces nouvelles responsabilités.
17.9 Une étape importante a donc été franchie. De tout temps, l'interprétation des cultures s'est avérée  féconde. C'est ainsi que l'UNESCO envisageait le moyen de servir la eulture en encourageant l'examen du  concours apporté par la civilisation arabe à la civilisation européenne. Ceci est d'autant plus important que  l'histoire a retenu le précieux concours qui a aidé les Européens à découvrir Aristote à travers les savants arabes  à l'époque de l'apogée de la civilisation culturelle de l'Espagne musulmane au Moyen Age. Il est à retenir  également dans ce contexte que, depuis le XIIIe siècle, l'Université de Padoue en Italie consacre une chaire à  l'enseignement du grand philosophe arabe Ibn Rushd, auteur de "L'entendement unique”, auquel Ernest Renan  à consacré tout un ouvrage intitulé " Averroès”.
17.10 Une déclaration importante vient d'être proclamée en ce qui concerne les droits de l'enfant. Cette  déclaration a été motivée par les nombreux abus dont les enfants ont été victimes dans différentes parties du  monde.
17.11 Ceci ouvre le chapitre à une maladie sociale qui a retenu l'attention des responsables de notre  Organisation : le danger des drogues et la maladie du sida, sans faire mention du désarroi d'une partie de la  jeunesse actuelle qui, désaxée, désemparée, ne se trouve nullement motivée par un noble idéal la rattachant aux  valeurs traditionnelles qui ont cimenté et façonné les générations précédentes.
17.12 Tous ces défis que nous avons énumérés iront en s'accentuant au cours des années à venir étant donné  la présence de tous ces réfugiés de l'Europe de l'Est qui sont dans l'attente fiévreuse des secours matériels et  moraux qu'ils sollicitent avec une impatience croissante. Un ouvrage vient d'être publié, dans lequel un  parallèle est fait entre le flux croissant des réfugiés qui peut menacer la stabilité du continent, et l'invasion des  barbares venant des steppes de la mer Noire et conduits par Alaric, qui ont ravagé Rome en l'an 400 après J.-C.  en mettant fin à la brillante civilisation romaine qui avait rayonné pendant des siècles dans le monde antique.  Evidemment, conclut l'auteur, M. Fontaine, cette dernière invasion est totalement exclue et mconcevable dans  notre monde moderne. Cet exemple invite à réfléchir, à envisager des mesures qui seraient adoptées  éventuellement pour parer au danger qui menace l'Europe. Il devient urgent, dès à présent, de promouvoir  l'esprit de solidarité qui ne laisse pas le soin de solutionner ce grave problème des réfugiés aux Etats, mais  nécessite le concours de différentes collectivités, des associations, des spécialistes qui ont déjà eu l'occasion  d'examiner le récours possible à des solutions pouvant satisfaire les besoins impérieux de ces pauvres  malheureux, victimes d'une conjoncture sociale dont ils ne sont pas responsables.
17.13 Ayant examiné tous ces défis, et étant donné la complexité des problèmes multiples qui requièrent des  solutions urgentes, l'Ordre de Malte ne manquera pas de prêter son concours en s'inspirant des expériences dont  il a été témoin et qui peuvent présenter une certaine similitude avec les problèmes déjà envisagés. En tout cas,  nous avons la ferme espérance que des éminents spécialistes, des grands savants animés d'une généreuse  volonté et des bénévoles prêts à s'engager dans la lutte contre la misère peuvent, d'une commune volonté,  affronter les obstacles, les surmonter et apporter au monde angoissé, en butte au désespoir quotidien, la flamme  de l'espoir.
17.14 Monsieur le Directeur général, au seuil de la vingt-sixième session de la Conférence générale de  l'UNESCO, au nom de l'Ordre souverain de Malte, je forme des voeux pour le succès de votre mission et des  efforts que vous déployez, ainsi que vos éminents collaborateurs, pour accomplir une si noble tâche.  L'expérience de notre collaboration mutuelle qui remonte à 1962 nous fait dire que ses résultats ne peuvent  qu'aller en s'intensifiant. Notre voeu est que des pays tels que les Etats-Unis et le Royaume-Uni nous rejoignent  également dans cette action. Monsieur le Directeur général, je vous remercie de tout coeur de l'appui que vous  avez bien voulu toujours nous accorder. Merci, Monsieur le Président.
18. The PRESIDENT:  Thank you Ambassador Soneriu. I now call on HE. Ms Marian Kamara, Ambassador of Sierra Leone  to Belgium and Permanent Delegate to UNESCO.
19.1 Ms KAMARA (Sierra Leone):  Mr President, Mr Chairman of the Executive Board, Mr Director-General, Your Excellencies,  distinguished delegates and observers, at the outset, permit me, Mr President, to associate my delegation with  the sentiments expressed by earlier speakers in offering you our warmest congratulations on your election to  this high office. We are confident that with your enlightened leadership, you will steer the affairs of this  Conference with all fairness and equanimity, striving constantly for consensus and constructive decisions which  will lead to fruitful results.
19.2 — Let me take this opportunity to commend the gracious manner in which the outgoing President of the  twenty-fifth session of the General Conference, Mr Anwar Ibrahim of Malaysia, carried out his onerous  responsibilities during his term of office. I am sure that all of us here appreciated the time and energy he  devoted to the smooth running of our Organization during this trying period.
19.3 I also wish to echo the satisfaction and deep appreciation already expressed by previous speakers  regarding the work done so far by the Chairman and members of the Executive Board, which makes our  deliberations here much easier. My delegation has no doubt whatsoever, Mr Chairman of the Executive Board,  that you will continue to discharge the responsibilities of this office with honour and dignity. UNESCO, we  believe, stands in need of your qualities, particularly at this time when the many, pressing problems of  yesteryear cry out for urgent solutions in our day.
19.4 1 also wish to express my delegation's apprectation of the excellent report given by the Director-  General which shows the quality and level of seriousness of the work undertaken by the Secretariat. As we  examine the Draft Programme and Budget for 1992-1993 presented to this august assembly for approval, my  delegation is convinced that UNESCO has not only provided us once more with a realistic appraisal of the  world situation, but also formulated for our consideration significant strategies and plans for action on themes  which are fundamental to our search for peace, international co-operation and development.
19.5 My delegation joins others in extending a warm welcome to the new Member States of UNESCO -  Tuvalu, Lithuania, Estonia and Latvia - which strengthens our clarion call for the universality of our  Organization.
196 Mr President, my delegation has read with deep interest the documents forwarded for this Conference,  including the recommendations of the Executive Board on the Draft Programme and Budget for 1992-1993. We  note that the Draft Programme and Budget, faithful to the spirit and content of the third Medtum-Term Plan
(1990-1995), has succeeded to a great extent to achieving the much-desired balance between reflection and  action.
19.7 In turning to the specific major programme areas in the draft programme and budget, 1 wish,  Mr President, to make the following comments on behalf of my delegation. My government recognizes  UNESCO's stand in rejecting the view that only a few should climb the heights of knowledge while the rest  should remain forever on the lower ranges of achievement. UNESCO's concern in Major Programme Area I  (Education and the future), has a special appeal to us, particularly in the developing countries. We recognize  and appreciate UNESCO's untiring efforts to alert Member States to the persistence of illiteracy and to arouse  the awareness of people throughout the world of the need to combat it. We all know that it 1s not just a question  of providing schools within reach and textbooks in sufficient quantity - it is also a question of maintaining the  quality and relevance of education, of reducing our drop-out and repetition rates, of providing adult education  and of helping those who have become literate to avoid relapse.
19.8 On the threshold of the twenty-first century, in this era of scientific and technological revolution, the  time is ripe for our governments to redouble their efforts to ensure that education systems are equipped to meet  these challenges. We, in Sierra Leone, realize that education is the most essential condition and powerful agent  for progress. Literacy for us is an enormous challenge, and as a democratic government we are duty bound to  do all that lies within our power to promote literacy education. Thus, my country's 1991 Mulü-Party  Constitution includes provisions which stipulate that the government shall strive lo eradicate illiteracy, and to  this end, shall direct its educational policy towards achieving free adult literacy programmes; free compulsory  basic education at primary and junior secondary school levels; and free senior secondary education as and when  practicable.
19.9 The problem of illiteracy is of fundamental concern to my government since it is at the root of our  socio-economic development. The top-most priority is that of reshaping the educational structure of the country  through new institutions with a different character and spirit, more in tune with the changing times and  changing needs of our society. In a bid to carry out government policies and strategies, to strengthen basic  education for all and to combat illiteracy, the Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport continues to extend and  improve primary education by developing new primary education reforms with a rural bias under the  UNESCO/UNDP project, by modifying existing primary schools in rural areas in order to make them into  multi-purpose community education centres, and by establishing village resource development centres. The new  structure to be implemented soon is the 6-3-3-4 educational system (six years of primary education, three years
550   of junior secondary education, three years of senior secondary education and four years at the tertiary level).  Within this structure new measures have been taken to make education more relevant to the developmental  requirements of our country. The principal objectives include more rapid expansion of primary and related  teacher education; greater alignment of secondary and post-secondary curricula with employment needs;  development of agro-based institutions particularly for school-leavers; greater emphasis on applied studies  leading to higher education and to more specialized skill development; and establishment of distance education  for those who cannot afford university education. In support of our efforts, UNESCO has sent a team of  education specialists who are working with our national experts on an education sector study. My delegation  would like to take this opportunity to express sincere thanks and appreciation to UNESCO on behalf of the  Government of Sierra Leone. Mr President, my delegation notes with satisfaction the intensification of the  Organization's activities in connection with the sciences and their applications to development as outlined in  Major Programme Area II (Science for progress and the environment). We believe that scientific and  technological co-operation is still an effective way of serving the cause of development and peace. Countries  such as ours face the urgent need to develop our natural resources if we are to survive the difficulties which  arise from the lack of balance between pressing material needs and present capabilities, and if we are to  minimize our dependence on the developed countries.
19.10 My delegation therefore welcomes the renewed emphasis on programmes such as environment and  natural resources management, and Science, technology and society. We call on UNESCO to take increasingly  into account in its science programmes the needs of the developing countries and to strengthen the training of  specialists at all levels, as they constitute the human tnfrastructure of the research and development systems of  our countries. In the past, training abroad and in particular in developed countries had numerous drawbacks  which encouraged brain drain and made the personnel so trained less responsive to the social and cultural  aspects of transfers of techniques. Networking in co-operation with African national institutions for training of  research workers has proved fruitful over the years, Sierra Leone also continues to be actively involved in  programmes like the International Hydrological Programme, Man and the Biosphere and ANSTI through its  national committees.
19.11 Major Programme Area III the main thrust of which is reflected in its title, Culture: past, present and  future’, is of particular relevance at a time when efforts to promote the values of culture and co-operation are  assuming even greater importance. My delegation therefore appreciates the fact that the draft programme and  budget calls upon UNESCO to encourage greater awareness and expression of cultural identities particularly in  the Plan of Action for the World Decade for Cultural Development (1988-1997). It is gratifying to note that  UNESCO continues to recognize the need to place culture at the centre of development, as reflected in the  proposed principles of action outlined in programmes such as culture for development, preservation and  enhancement of the cultural heritage and enrichment of cultural identities. We also note and support the  observance of 31 May as the World Day for Cultural Development which Sierra Leone has pledged to celebrate  with appropriate activities in the future.
19.12 Mr President, my delegation is heartened to note that the lines of programme concentration in Major  Programme Area IV (Communication in the service of humanity) are in the true spirit of the new  communication strategy formulated and adopted at the twenty-fifth session of the General Conference. We  appreciate the fact that the main focus of UNESCO's action is to ensure the free flow of information, both  within countries and internationally; wider and better-balanced dissemination; and the strengthening of  communication capacities in the developing countries so that they are better integrated into the communication  process. We also note and appreciate the steps taken to establish the International Programme for the  Development of Communication (IPDC) as the universally recognized forum for concerted effort for effective  implementation of the third Medium-Term Plan; and the setting up of a new Communication, Information and  Informatics Sector, combining communication, technology, information and informatics. The reinforcement of  IPDC in terms of provision of additional funds from UNESCO's regular budget to supplement the resources of  the Special Account, is also an encouraging trend. Activities which would be of immediate help to us in the  developing countries include the strengthening of our communication infrastructures, the training of personnel,  and the achievement of a free and more balanced flow of information in the world.
19.13 My delegation welcomes the general trend of Major Programme Area V (The social and human  sciences in a changing world) and its programme structure, as well as the proposals for strengthening the  institutional development of the social and human sciences and research and teaching activities in Member  States. We believe that the priority given to strengthening national capacities for advanced training, research
14   and information and to regional and international co-operation in this field will help tremendously to reduce  existing imbalances.
19.14 Mr President, my delegation appreciates the efforts that have been and are still being made concerning  UNESCO's contribution to prospective studies and to strategies related to development in Major Programme  Area VI. For us in developing countries, development is still basically linked with the enhancement of human  capabilities. It is, however, sad to note that our debt-burdened countries have been constrained to curtail  investment, reduce expenditure on education, health and other social services and impose deflationary policies  on our economies, which result in a decline in investments in human development. UNESCO's concern to  rethink development and to design new strategies which take account of what is distinctive in each nation's way  of life and culture becomes more urgent today. We are reassured that a large part of the funds in this major  programme area will be allocated to the development of human resources, to the cultural and environmental  dimensions of development and to the formulation of national and regional development strategies.
19.15 Mr President, all of us present here should join hands in strengthening the capacity of UNESCO which  sceks to found peace on human reason and intelligence, through education, science, culture and  communication, as outlined in proposed principles of action in Major Programme Area VII (UNESCO's  contribution to peace, human rights and the elimination of all forms of discrimination). We need to give  support to strategies for developing action programmes which underline UNESCO's determination to take a  global view of the world situation and to intensify the struggle against prejudice, intolerance, racism and  apartheid in order to foster peace, international understanding, human rights and the rights of peoples. We  welcome the emphasis on activities relating to the promotion of democracy, to human rights teaching, to  combating violence against women and to improving the equality of the sexes.
19.16 In conclusion, Mr President, I wish to reaffirm my delegation's support for the comprehensive  recommendations of the Executive Board on the Draft Programme and Budget for 1992-1993 adopted at its
136th session. We believe that at a time like this when the needs of Member States, especially the developing  countries in the fields of education, science, culture and communication are more urgent than ever before, it is  in the interests of the world community as a whole to combine in strengthening UNESCO in every possible way  so that our Organization can help us meet the challenges of national and international development and world  peace through international co-operation. It is in this spirit that I should like to appeal to all Member States  whose representatives are assembled here to adopt the Draft Programme and Budget for 1992-1993 by  consensus as a measure of solidarity and international co-operation in the urgent tasks that lie ahead of us all. I  thank you.

22. The PRESIDENT:  Now, ladies and gentlemen, this is the last day of the general policy debate. We have 39 speakers on  the list, and unless we observe the time-limit, we are likely to extend until tomorrow morning. I would like to  make a final desperate appeal to the remaining speakers to try and observe the time-limit. Our next speaker is  the Hon. Gloria Henry, Minister of Education and head of the delegation of Trinidad and Tobago.
23.1 Ms HENRY (Trinidad and Tobago):  Mr President, Mr Director-General, Excellencies, ladies and gentlemen, may I join in congratulating  you on your election to the presidency. I also wish to add my welcome to the new members of UNESCO,  Tuvalu, Estonia, Lithuania and Latvia.
23.2 Trinidad and Tobago was pleased to play host in September 1991 to the Director-General of UNESCO,  Mr Federico Mayor. This visit proved to be highly significant for our country in that it heightened national  awareness of the scope and importance of the work being done by UNESCO. Undoubtedly, this visit, which was  preceded by a visit to Jamaica, also served to sensitize the head of the Organization to the distinctive social,  cultural and economic realities of the Caribbean region. This region comprises 13 of the Member States in  Electoral Group III of UNESCO, Latin America and the Caribbean, but has not enjoyed proportionate  representation on the Executive Board. Currently, we occupy one seat on the Board and this will be vacated at  the end of the twenty-sixth session of the General Conference. The Caribbean, therefore will be seeking to  maintain this seat and to secure at least two others within Electoral Group III in 1993,
23.3 — This determination to gain proportionate representation on the Executive Board is backed up by a  determination to play a more active role in UNESCO affairs generally. To this end, we shall also be seeking to  be elected to the subsidiary bodies of UNESCO. Trinidad and Tobago, for example, will be seeking election to  the International Co-ordinating Council of the Man and the Biosphere Programme and to the International  Hydrological Programme. While the Caribbean as a whole is striving to make a greater contribution to the work  of UNESCO through representation on the Executive Board and other bodies, Trinidad and Tobago is at the  same time reviewing the structure and role of its National Commission in recognition of the indispensable need  for a strong National Commission to support our activities in UNESCO affairs.
23.4 With respect to the Draft Programme and Budget for 1992-1993, Mr President, Trinidad and Tobago  supports the basic thrust of Major Programme Area I (Education and the future). Since 1988 ministers of  education in the Caribbean have expressed concern about some major issues in education, such as declining  functional literacy, inadequate skills in numeracy, and insufficient education for living. Ministers recognized  that attempts were being made to respond to some of these concerns, but that interventions were sporadic,  lacking in a multidisciplinary approach and therefore incapable of making a fundamental impact on the  education process. Moreover, given the commonality in the educational systems of CARICOM Member States  and the fact that concerns are applicable across the region, it was agreed that the identification of the way  forward was amenable to a regional process and approach. Some of the regional concerns being addressed  relate to innovations and new curricula, language and communications, teacher education, educational  administration, and policy planning and analysis, which includes the issue of the financing of education.
23.5 The region is also working towards the establishment of a cost-effective system for the publication of  educational materials and a mechanism for determining equivalency of educational credentials.
23.6 With respect to the proposals for research, Trinidad and Tobago has a special interest in strengthening  its research capabilities in education. In the present context of scarce resources, my country, like other  territories in the region, needs data in order to make informed decisions about education for the future. The  value of research cannot be overemphasized and Trinidad and Tobago is therefore pleased to observe, in Major  Programme Area I, a provision for research in education that includes the Caribbean. My country is also  anxious to modernize its management systems and will be seeking UNESCO's assistance to secure funding for  its computer-assisted management information system in education (CAMISL).
23.7 In keeping with the growing importance of international education Trinidad and Tobago will be  including in the philosophical framework of its new education plan goals which reflect the value of co-  operation with the international community. To this end, we strongly support the activities of the UNESCO  Associated Schools Project and UNESCO Clubs. As an indication of the importance our country attaches to  international education, Trinidad and Tobago has offered to host in 1992 the third international consultation on  the interregional project to enhance the multiplier effect of the UNESCO Associated Schools Project.
23.8 Trinidad and Tobago has for some time now been seeking to strengthen its science and technology  capability as a support for human resource development. We have established institutions such as the National  Institute of Higher Education Research, Science and Technology (NIHERST) and the Institute of Marine  Affairs (IMA). Efforts are now being made to set up a national science centre to stimulate interest in science in  a hands-on, interactive environment. We note the resources proposed in the programme and budget in the areas  of science and technology popularization and repair and maintenance, both of which are areas of interest to  Trinidad and Tobago. In our view there is a need for closer collaboration between UNESCO's science and  technology sector and its education sector to enhance the Organization's ability to respond to the needs of the  Caribbean in the development of programmes for science and technology education at the primary and  secondary levels as well as for teacher education.
23.9 The all-encompassing nature of Major Programme Area III can accommodate all the cultural concerns  of Caribbean countries. Trinidad and Tobago, for its part, is sensitive to the influence which UNESCO's major  programme on the World Decade for Cultural Development can have on strengthening the role of culture in  development in our country. The year 1992, which has been designated the Year of the Arts in Trinidad and  Tobago, will therefore see a cultural explosion in our country and at centre stage will be CARIFESTA V.
23.10 CARIFESTA V is the fifth Festival of Arts of the Caribbean in 25 years. It will bring together, in  Trinidad and Tobago, some 2,000 artists from the English-, Spanish-, French- and Dutch-speaking countries.  During CARIFESTA, hosts and visitors will participate in a historic fusion of the various fragments from  which have evolved the spiritual power of the Caribbean people and the unique rhythms of the Caribbean  islands. Naturally, we will be anxious to document this rich cultural experience in print and audio-visual media  and look forward to UNESCO's support in this regard. We also wish to encourage a physical UNESCO  presence during CARIFESTA V as a symbol of the close relationship we hope to continue to foster between  UNESCO and the people of the Caribbean,
23.11 Mr President, 1992 will also be the year in which the Columbus Quincentennial will be observed with  special interest in many countries of the world. Trinidad and Tobago will be participating from the perspective  of the Encounter between Two Worlds’. Among the activities planned is an international conference which  embodies the broad concept of ‘Centuries of Achievement’. This Conference, another avenue for co-operation  with UNESCO, will address some of the major achievements of the English-speaking Caribbean and include  our contribution to world thought and the evolution of Caribbean society. This coming together of Caribbean  scholars will crystallize emerging concepts contained in a dynamic Caribbean philosophy.
23.12 In the area of communication the priority needs of the Caribbean are infrastructural: the strenthening  of media systems and the improvement and increase of indigenous production, particularly with respect to  television. UNESCO funding for communication technologies in the Caribbean is insufficient. We strongly feel  that there should be increased budgetary support for integrated strategies for communication development and  endogenous production of programmes and materials.
23.13 As regards Major Programme Area V (The social and human sciences in a changing world), it must be  pointed out, Mr President, that there is no UNESCO specialist for this area available within the Caribbean.  Trinidad and Tobago, therefore, requests the provision of such an expert along with the necessary funding for  the post.
23.14 One important institution initiated and supported by UNESCO under this programme is the  Consortium Graduate School for Social Sciences, which is based in Jamaica. This institution is considered to be  making a significant impact within the region. It offers postgraduate multidisciplinary programmes and is  conducting research on issues directly related to the sustainable development of the region. In 1992, the  Caribbean region will be looking forward to the co-operation of UNESCO in securing continued UNDP funding  for this institution.
23.15 With respect to the allocation for Major Programme Area VI, Trinidad and Tobago recommends an  increase in UNESCO's contribution to prospective studies and to strategies concerned with development,  particularly for training activities. It is to be noted that the Caribbean region has received no allocation of funds  in this area and we request that this situation be rectified. Mr President, our country has a special interest in the  provisions of this programme regarding the improvement of the status of women. Of particular concern to us is  the fact that women are often the victims of domestic violence. In response to this problem, a bill on domestic  violence was passed recently in our parliament. Our Ministry of Social Development and Family Services  intends to produce two documentary films entitled ‘Shattered’ and Roles’ for use in educating the public on  certain aspects of domestic violence. This project proposal has already been submitted to the Secretariat for  UNESCO's assistance in securing budgetary funding.
23.16 Concerning efforts to restructure the Organization for increased effectiveness and efficiency, Trinidad  and Tobago supports the steps being taken towards decentralization. We note that in effect decentralization has  already begun with the addition of representational responsibilities to three of the regional advisers to the  Caribbean. We, like our neighbours, are very concerned about the actual effectiveness of this appointment of  UNESCO representatives since they now bear greater responsibility, but must operate within the same  budgetary and staff support constraints. If decentralization is to be meaningful, Mr President, it must be  accompanied by an increased decentralization of funds to each representative for the Caribbean and by, at least,  the strengthening of the General Service staff, especially in matters of financial control and office  administration.
23.17 Mr President, Trinidad and Tobago, and indeed the Caribbean as a whole, recognizes the importance  of UNESCO's General Conference as we review our developmental thrust and devise programmes to meet  present and anticipated needs. While we recognize the great value of the Organization's programmes and the  contribution they make to the development process at the national and regional levels, we are also very mindful  of the value of our own contributions, intellectual and otherwise, to the work of UNESCO. We are, therefore,  currently taking the necessary steps to ensure permanent Caribbean representation in Paris to service Caribbean  membership in UNESCO. The Caribbean community is serviced by the CARICOM secretariat which is  mandated by standing committees of ministers responsible for the various sectors. At present, Mr President, I  have the honour of serving as Chairperson of the Standing Committee of Ministers Responsible for Education.  . We are pleased that, for the first time, the CARICOM secretariat is represented at UNESCO's General  Conference. This is an indication, Mr President, that over the years the ties between UNESCO and the  Caribbean are being strengthened at the national and regional levels. This is in keeping with the spirit of  rebirth that is now sweeping across the Organization and in which the Caribbean and Trinidad and Tobago in  particular intend to play a significant role. Thank you, Mr President.
24. The PRESIDENT:  I thank the head of the delegation of Trinidad and Tobago. I now give the floor to the Hon. Armoogum  Parsuraman, Minister of Education and Science and head of the delegation of Mauritius.
25.1 Mr PARSURAMAN (Mauritius):  Mr President, Mr Director-General, distinguished heads of delegations, delegates, ladies and  gentlemen, it 1s heartening to take part in such an august gathering of leading personalities of the educational,  cultural and scientific world, among the representatives of 163 Member States, in an assembly which is so ably  guided in its deliberations by you, Mr President. | would like at once to pay tribute to the vision and remarkable  perspicacity of the Director-General. His leadership has revitalized the Organization and created a new  international consciousness about education, through his outstanding dedication to the cause of humanism and  his personal attention to the development of Third World countries.
25.2 Mr President, the past two years have been an exceptionally eventful period of reflection on education.  The World Conference on Education for All, an unprecedented international event, laid a new and bold  emphasis on education as a central requirement in the development process. A deep awareness has been created  of its essentiality for the progress and advancement of humanity. Education has indeed been placed in the front  line of national and international priorities.
25.3 Let me recall here the appeal made by the Director-General at the World Conference, when he called  on governments, private and public bodies, non-governmental organizations and educationists, to join hands in  what he termed a 'grand alliance’, in the task of achieving basic education for all, pointing out that education  for all needs the contribution of all. To quote his words: 'If we combine visiun with pragmatism, political will
14   with economic resourcefulness, international solidarity with national commitment, the expertise of educators  with the fresh contributions of the media, science and technology, the business community, voluntary  organizations and many others - then, and probably only then, the struggle to bring education to all can be won.  Everybody can contribute, everybody can share in forging a brighter future’.
254 For countries in the process of economic development, education bears a greater significance. It  represents liberation from the fetters of underdevelopment and stagnation, freedom from poverty, ignorance and  backwardness and the hope of a healthier, better and qualitative life-style. It is a means to acquire basic human  dignity. But developing countries also labour under impressive constraints - the main ones being financial  limitations, the shortage of qualified human resources, problems related to the brain drain, the impossibility of  keeping pace with technological and scientific developments, and the prohibitive costs of technical training and  higher education. The translation of their intentions into reality is often hampered by harassing poverty, disease  and the balance of payments burden. Increasing budgetary allocations to education is neither easy nor  practicable for many of them. National efforts thus have to be strengthened by international solidarity, while  there is a necessity to make choices and to review the allocation of resources.
25.5 The developing countries have consequently welcomed with encouragement the renewed commitment  of UNICEF, ILO, the World Bank, UNDP and other donor agencies to channel more of their resources towards  education, in association with UNESCO and other important international bodies. This sign of international  development co-operation is a motivating force for developing countries in working out their own plans for  basic education.
25.6 In this context, Mr President, I believe we all welcome another very important move in the direction of  increased international solidarity. I am referring to the statement made last week by the International Monetary  Fund (IMF), which proposed a strategy of mutually agreed global defence spending reductions, expected to save  from $140 to $180 billion a year worldwide, a resource which could be used for productive investment,  including investment in human resources development. As the Administrator of UNDP pointed out at the  Jomtien Conference, We can put all our children in primary schools by spending an additional $4 to $5 billion  every year over the next ten years. This represents only two days expenditure on arms by the industrialized  nations and only one week of military spending in the developing countries’.
25.7 Having quoted the UNDP Administrator, I cannot refrain from quoting Federico Mayor - the poet  rather than the Director-General of UNESCO - who in one of his poems, entitled 'Aguafuertes’ exclaims:  '1Cudntos pupitres, aulas, libros, libres, podrian salir de estos fusiles!".
25.8 Mr President, it is a matter of satisfaction to us that Mauritius has made appreciable progress in  education. Its remarkable industrial and economic development, in a democratic and free environment, is  attributed mostly to education. After 24 years of sovereignty, we have maintained free education at the primary,  secondary and tertiary levels. Education is available to all without any form of discrimination; the primary  school enrolment rate is around 98 per cent; textbooks, food supplements and medical care are provided free of  charge to schoolchildren; free technical training is available in a number of areas relevant to Mauritian needs;  disparities between schools have been reduced and primary schools are accessible in the remotest parts of the  island; the curriculum is consistently renewed and updated, and continuous training programmes are mounted  to update pedagogical methods.
25.9 In a small country such as our own, with a democratic tradition and a heterogeneous population,  deriving its roots from Africa, Asia and Europe, education policies have to heed the diversity of the population.  For historical and social reasons, education is considered as the main avenue of social mobility, and the  pressure and demands on it are forceful. The education system has to respond to new types of requirements. It  is called upon to provide trained and qualified professionals and technicians and at the same time to transmit  the basic human values, to play an active role in research, to provide lifelong and continuing education and to  maintain peaceful and harmonious coexistence.
25.10 It was with the worldwide objective of education for all by the year 2000 in mind that the Government  of Mauritius undertook the formulation of a comprehensive educational programme, which has become the  nation's Master Plan for Education for All. Our past experience had shown that policies imposed by external  agencies do not necessarily satisfy the social, cultural and economic needs of the country. This time, therefore,  we have attempted to draw up a programme that matches the Mauritian context, embodying the aspirations of
556   the people, and response to local needs. We chose our international partners, particularly UNESCO and UNDP,  who provided prompt and competent support, under our own national guidance.
25.11 We have identified the problems in a democratic approach, with widespread consultations with all  partners - students, teachers, parents, heads of schools, education authorities and non-governmental  organizations. This exercise proved to be a fruitful effort in building up national consensus on the policy  measures proposed.
25.12 Thus, our Master Plan for Education reflects the views and suggestions of the people of Mauritius as a  whole. The preparation of the plan is leading to the adoption of a far-reaching education reform which extends  basic education from six to nine years for all. Major objectives are to ensure that each child reaches an agreed  standard of basic education; that the inequalities are reduced; and that the quality of education is improved at  all levels. The plan envisages structural reforms to make the education system more efficient and provide  adequate avenues for children of different abilities. Our new education strategy is concerned with the promotion  of technical and vocational training, with research in selected areas of national concern, with the restructuring  and expansion of tertiary education. One very important aspect of the plan is the review of the role and status of  teachers.
25.13 Mr President, may I share briefly with you our experiénce in working out our new policy and the  Master Plan. It is an experience of good international co-operation, with UNESCO and UNDP, and also ILO,  the World Bank, the African Development Bank and others. I would like to acknowledge the innovative,  professional and efficient contribution by the dynamic Sector Analysis Division in the Bureau for Co-ordination  of Operational Activities at UNESCO. Working out our Master Plan has been essentially a national  undertaking, with international experts joining our national working group from time to time to provide  expertise and to be catalysts for the policy dialogue. Through this approach, the Master Plan work was  intersectoral, interdisciplinary and carried out in an inter-agency manner. We would like to encourage  UNESCO to pursue its operational work along these lines.
25.14 The Government of Mauritius has allocated substantial financial resources to the education sector,  thereby demonstrating that education ranks high on its priority list. Thanks to the assistance of UNESCO and  UNDP, a donors’ consultative meeting is to be held on 5 and 6 November here at Headquarters, to present the  Plan to donors and mobilize resources in terms of grants, technical assistance and loans, to supplement local  funding.
25.15 In this perspective, we strongly support the budgetary increase in the programme and budget proposed  for 1992-1993, for literacy and adult education and technical and vocational education. We fully endorse the  emphasis laid on the mobilization of extra-budgetary resources for the development of basic and engineering  sciences. We consider as a progressive measure the priority attached to environment education, informatics and  research in science and technology. Developing countries stand to benefit from such support, given the high  costs of research and training and the advance of the industrialized countries in these sectors. We commend  UNESCO on its support for research, documentation and training. Our own projects for the expansion of our  university and the improvement of science laboratories in secondary schools are in line with the objectives set  by the Organization.
25.16 On behalf of the Government of Mauritius, I fully endorse UNESCO's proposed programme and  budget and its undertaking that special attention should be given to providing assistance to developing  countries and underprivileged social sectors.
25.17 As a representative of a free, democratic country which has a diverse population, and stands as an  example of peaceful coexistence . :  (The speaker continued in French)  Je voudrais, et vous me permettrez ici, Excellences, Mesdames, Messieurs, d'ouvrir une parenthèse en français,  pour rappeler que dans cette même salle, dans un discours prononcé lors de l'ouverture de la deuxième  Conférence des Nations Unies sur les pays les moins avancés, le Président de la République française,  M. François Mitterrand, déclarait : "Indissociables (...) sont le développement et la démocratie. Le  développement s'appuie sur le respect des droits fondamentaux, la liberté d'aller et de venir, la loi au service de  tous. J'ai visité récemment l'île Maurice. C'est un exemple qui montre que l'on peut conjuguer ces deux termes :
14   développement et démocratie”. A l'occasion de cette même visite officielle dans mon pays, s'adressant au peuple  mauricien à travers son Premier ministre, Sir Anerood Jugnauth, il lui disait : Je dois vous le dire, vous nous  offrez un exemple rare, votre démocratie est déjà très ancienne, vous l'avez encore affermie depuis l'heure de  votre indépendance. Vous n'ignorez rien de ses joutes, vous en vivez sans doute, parfois, les inconvénients.  Nous pouvons parler entre gens qui savent de quoi ils parlent. Mais à côté de ces inconvénients que  d'avantages ! ... Le respect des droits fondamentaux, la liberté d'expression, de communication, la liberté  d'aller et de venir, la recherche de l'égalité, la loi au service de tous, et, rêve peut-être plus moral que politique,  la fraternité sans laquelle il serait vain d'essayer de vivre durablement ensemble. Au-delà de vos différences qui  existent comme partout ailleurs, vous pratiquez une démocratie scrupuleuse. Les lois de la démocratie dirigent  votre société.”  (L'orateur reprend en anglais)
25.18 It is, then, natural that we strongly endorse the UNESCO special project entitled: ‘Contribution to the  elimination of apartheid..." and its programme of activities towards a post-apartheid society in South Africa.  International organizations have a crucial and decisive role to play in the improving the lot of our oppressed  brothers and sisters in South Africa.
25.19 The international consciousness aroused by the World Conference on Education for All, International  Literacy Year and the Convention on the Rights of the Child already marks a salutary step forward. Mauritius  has assumed the challenge. We want to feel that in our search for the betterment of the future of our children,  we are not isolated; that the whole world is behind us and that we stand as one against ignorance. I have the  conviction that we can move ahead steadily and confidently; and I would like to conclude, Mr President, by  quoting Pandit Jawaharlal Nehru: "The future can only hold out its promise to us, if we learn to march together".  Thank you.
26. The PRESIDENT:  I thank the head of the delegation of Mauritius. In giving the floor now to the Hon. Nahas Angula,  Minister of Education and Culture and head of the delegation of Namibia, may I add that his country is taking  part in this debate for the first time in history: our welcome is thus a double one.
27.1 Mr ANGULA (Namibia):  Mr President of the General Conference, Mr Chairman of the Executive Board, Mr Director-General,  distinguished heads of delegation and delegates, ladies and gentlemen, the Namibian delegation appreciates the  fact that one of Africa's illustrious sons has been elevated to the high office of President of our General  Conference. I should like to extend our congratulations and full support to him and to wish him every success  in his arduous task. Distinguished delegates, Namibia is both an old and new member of UNESCO. An old  member because it was represented in this august body for a number of years by the United Nations Council for  Namibia; a new member in that for the first time, it is represented today by the government elected and  established by the will of the people. It is therefore my singular honour and privilege, as I welcome new fellow  members who have joined UNESCO during this session, at the same time to express sincere thanks to the  members of the former United Nations Council for Namibia who effectively represented the interests of our  people at UNESCO. I hope that these States will continue to support the new government in establishing its  presence in the governing bodies of UNESCO. In this regard I would like to mention that we shall be seeking  representation on the Executive Board as well as on the Headquarters Committee, on the Council of the  International Bureau of Education, and on the Intergovernmental Committee for the Return of Cultural  Property to its Countries of Origin or its Restitution in Case of Illicit Appropriation. I believe that Namibia,  being the child of the United Nations, deserves full representation in the United Nations family.
27,2 Mr President, for my delegation this is indeed a memorable occasion: after long and hard years of  struggle, Namibia is finally participating in the twenty-sixth session of the General Conference of UNESCO as  a full member of the Organization and a sovereign independent State. Recalling the commitment of UNESCO  and its members to the cause of Namibia's independence, my country gratefully recognizes the moral and vital  humanitarian support, technical and other contributions that, together with the dedication and sacrifices of the  Namibian people, finally culminated in political independence on 21 March 1990. Having achieved this long-  cherished ideal, the young Namibian nation has to face the challenges of social and economic development.  The basic human rights and principles of freedom, democracy and a meaningful life as enshrined in our  Constitution need to be translated into practical and sustainable programmes for the benefit of our population.  Above all, the development of Namibia's human potential is recognized as a decisive factor in achieving our
558   goal of building a unified, democratic, peaceful and self-sustaining society. To this end, we have had to  determine our national priorities and critically examine our educational situation in the light of the historic  apartheid legacy, cultural diversity, economic realities and development goals. Supported by consultancies from  UNESCO and other United Nations Specialized Agencies, and with assistance from various international and  bilateral sources, a picture is emerging of a nation determined to embark vigorously upon its own development,  but a nation in dire need of support from the international community and its agencies,
27.3 In the spirit of the World Declaration on Education for All and the Framework for Action to Meet  Basic Learning Needs (with Namibia fully subscribing to both documents), and aware that the neglected  majority of Namibians were denied access to quality education by the previous colonial regimes, the broad  educational goals of the government as regards education include: improved access to basic education; equity in  the distribution of educational resources and services; improvement of learning achievement; design and  development of relevant curricula to ensure personal and social development; equal opportunity to higher  education and employment; broadening the literacy base and provision of adult education in a system providing  lifelong learning opportunities; a renewed commitment to special education; emphasis on science and  technology education; and enhancing and developing Namibian culture and strengthening cultural ties with the  international community.
27.4 Ladies and gentlemen, in the first year of independence the efforts, energies and initiatives of the  government have been directed towards the creation of strong foundations from which programmes could be  initiated and activities launched to achieve national objectives. While gratefully acknowledging assistance from  the international community during this initial phase, Namibia has now reached the critical point of project  design and implementation. We have the vision and commitment to embark upon the road towards becoming a  proud and self-supporting nation. However, due to the massive social deficit left behind by apartheid, we need  now and in the immediate future the financial and technical assistance to translate ideals into realities.
27.5 Mr President, turning to Namibia's relationship with UNESCO, it needs to be reaffirmed that we fully  share the Organization's principles and ideals and support its work in the interests of peace, international  understanding and human development. Namibia is determined to play its part and to contribute (on the  national, regional and international levels) towards the solution of major challenges, be it in the fields of peace,  development, culture, science or protection of the environment. Since independence Namibia has been actively  involved in regional UNESCO conferences (including MINEDAF VI in Dakar, Senegal in July 1991). An  excellent working relationship has been established with the UNESCO office in Windhoek and the Namibian  Government has the highest appreciation for the co-operation and genuine support this office has demonstrated  since its establishment. We are firmly committed to strengthening established ties and increasing our  involvement in the regional and international activities under the aegis of UNESCO.
276 In this spirit Namibia wishes to congratulate the Director-General, the Executive Board and the  Secretariat on the Draft Programme and Budget for 1992-1993. We congratulate you on the clarity of  presentation of aims, objectives and programme details and budgets provided in this exemplary working  document. In broad terms, two general aspects need comment from my delegation. Firstly, Namibia appreciates  and fully supports the identification of three categories of beneficiaries whose urgent needs and expectations  represent special target groups for priority action, namely: the least developed countries (LDCs) including  Namibia; the Member States of the Africa region, of which Namibia is part; and women, who account for more  than half Namibia's population. This represents a realistic way of targeting resources to areas and groups whose  social and economic structures are in danger of total collapse, unless outside assistance is made available to  bolster national development initiatives.
27.7 The proposed reduction in expenditure to be achieved by streamlining operations, internal savings  through the redeployment of staff and other measures, are fully supported. As savings are to be redirected to  high-priority activities and particularly to the urgent needs of the most disadvantaged countries, this will truly  benefit Namibia and other Member States facing crisis situations.
27.8 Focusing on the seven major programme areas, general support is expressed for the various  programmes and programme actions. The Namibian delegation's position on specific issues will be clarified in  the sessions of the various commissions. There is a need, however, to highlight a few areas of special  significance for Namibia and the southern Africa subregion.
27.9 Major Programme Area I (Education and the future) is of particular importance to us for its strong  emphasis on developing the full educational capacities of Member States. Programmes aimed at improving  access to schools, enhancing the quality of primary education, literacy and adult education programmes,  strengthening science and technology in developing countries through appropriate education, fostering the  growth and the quality of higher education - all of these programmes are in line with the aspirations and  objectives of my country. Namibia can greatly benefit from support in these areas, with particular needs  associated with the education of marginalized groups. In higher education we face the task of restructuring and  strengthening our university. In this Namibia can benefit from assistance in the training of university  administrators and various other programmes.
27.10 Major Programme Area II (Science for progress and the environment), focusing on science, technology  and environmental issues in fostering development is of particular significance for all LDCs like Namibia.  Although generally rich in natural resources, our capacity to harness natural assets for the improvement of the  quality of life of our population is still sadly lacking. Namibia is heavily dependent on optimal utilization and  the responsible management of its natural resources, while having to cope with a fragile semi-arid  environment. Support in the development of marine science and marine resource management will prove vital  to Namibia's economy, while programmes on hydrology and water resource management are literally a matter  of life or death for our rural population.
27.11 Major Programme Area IN (Culture: past, present and future) earns our full support as an integral part  of man's full existence and a meaningful life. Since gaining independence, Namibia has entered into bilateral  cultural agreements with a number of countries, thus promoting and participating in cultural exchanges and  mutual appreciation of cultures. Internally, a high premium is placed on the preservation and enhancement of  our cultural heritage. Support from UNESCO and its Member States is sought for the return or restitution of  cultural property removed from Namibia during its dark century of colonialism. Namibia could further benefit  from and participate in programmes for the promotion of performing arts in Africa and the strengthening of  national and regional capacities for textbook production.
27.12 Under Major Programme Area IV (Communication in the service of humanity) Namibia fully supports  the free flow of information and is indeed proud of the freedom of its press. There is, however, in this area a  pressing need for full involvement, particularly of rural populations, in the development process - an aim only  to be achieved by means of effective communication. The linkage between communication and development is  undisputable, and the communication needs of Namibia and fellow Member States in the southern African  region deserve international support as a prerequisite for development.
27.13 Under Major Programme Area V (The social and human sciences in a changing world), the planned  activities in this area are broadly supported by Namibia, with particular emphasis on women, youth and  population initiatives.
27.14 Major Programme Area VI (UNESCO's contribution to prospective studies and to strategies concerned  with development) may at this moment present the greatest challenge to Namibia and all other developing  countries. Programmes aimed at developing human resources as the basis for economic and social development  fully deserve the support of all Member States.
27.15 Major Programme Area VII (UNESCO's contribution to peace, human rights and the elimination of all  forms of discrimination) touches on an area of deep concern for the Namibian population, and enjoys its fullest  support. Emerging from a history of suffering under apartheid, and being a next-door neighbour to South  Africa with close economic and infrastructural ties, Namibia is uniquely placed among UNESCO Member  States to make a valuable contribution to programmes and activities related to policies, practices and needs of a  post-apartheid South Africa.
27.16 Namibia further expresses its support for transverse themes and programmes targeting women, youth,  general information, statistical programmes and services.
27.17 Mr President, Namibia reiterates its sincere appreciation for UNESCO's and its Member States’ role in  assisting and support to our struggle for political independence. With that target achieved, our goal-posts have  shifted on to the fields of economic and social development, thus posing new and demanding challenges. While  assuring UNESCO of our full co-operation, involvement and contributions towards achieving the ideals and  objectives of this Organization, Namibia pledges its full support for the initiatives taken by the Director-
560   General, especially within the context of Priority: Africa and the World Declaration on Education for All and  its Plan of Action. We look forward to mutual benefit within the ambit of co-operation with UNESCO. Thank  you.
28. The PRESIDENT:  I thank the head of the delegation of Namibia. Ladies and gentlemen, the afternoon meeting is  adjourned until 3 o'clock.  La séance est levée 2 13 h.15  The meeting rose at 1.15 p.m.  Se levanta la sesién a las 13.15
